Cristiano Ronaldo: 9 of his international records that will never be broken

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Cristiano Ronaldo has called time on his incredible international career with Portugal and will leave behind a legacy hard to match.

Ronaldo is bowing out from international duty after 234 caps and 146 goals for Portugal. He won the Euros with them in 2016 and went on to triumph twice in the UEFA Nations League.

A World Cup evaded him, but Ronaldo made his mark at that competition too over the years, scoring 11 times across six tournaments.

As one of the best players in history, his efforts in a Portugal shirt will stand the test of time in the record books.

He boasts plenty of records in international football – and these ones might never be beaten.

There are only three men with more than 100 international goals, but Ronaldo is way out in front. He quits 20 ahead of Lionel Messi, who also retired from international football this year, and 38 ahead of Ali Daei.

The active footballer closest to Ronaldo’s record is Romelu Lukaku on 94, but at the age of 33, it’s hard to see him catching up. The same goes for 38-year-old Robert Lewandowski on 92.

We’re pretty sure Harry Kane has plenty more goals for England in the tank, but enough to overtake Ronaldo? He’d need more than 50, so it’s a long shot.

If anyone stands half a chance it’s probably Erling Haaland on 65 goals, but his chances might depend on how many major tournaments Norway qualify for. It’s easy to forget the 2026 World Cup was their first major tournament since Euro 2000.

There’s Kylian Mbappe on 67 for France as well, but it makes you realise how impressive Ronaldo’s record is when you consider he’s not even halfway to reaching it.

Ronaldo has been able to reach those lofty goalscoring heights by playing more than anyone else in the international game.

His 234 caps for Portugal are 26 more than the amount Messi managed for Argentina. Third on the list is the still-active Luka Modric, 28 behind his former Real Madrid teammate but nearing the end at the age of 41.

Qatar’s Hassan Al-Haydos is the next nearest active challenger on 193 caps. The Al Sadd captain is only 35, so might end up not too far off, but it’ll still be a tall order.

Ronaldo played for his country for 23 years, after all, and that longevity is going to be hard for anyone to match.

Talking of longevity, Ronaldo hasn’t been wasting time at the tail end of his career. He actually scored nearly twice as many goals for Portugal after turning 30 than before.

It probably makes sense, with Ronaldo playing more centrally as the years went on after starting out as a winger.

In total, he scored 94 goals for Portugal after turning 30, compared to 52 before.

Unsurprisingly, it’s Messi behind him in the ranking, with an impressive 68 goals for Argentina after his 30th birthday.

But Ronaldo’s record will take some beating. Only four other players have ever scored 94 international goals, let alone after the age of 30. This one’s probably sticking.

Similar principle here. Ronaldo scored in 96 of the Portugal games he played in. Only he, Messi and Daei have reached that amount of goals in international football regardless of whether they were scoring braces or hat-tricks.

Ronaldo scored against a grand total of 49 different countries for Portugal, meaning he had three more victims on the international stage than Messi.

An ever-expanding World Cup might help, but it’s hard to imagine anyone scoring against 50 different countries.

It’d probably have to be someone from Europe or Africa, since those confederations have the most members. But they’d still be relying on the luck of the draw in qualifiers for tournaments.

Ronaldo scored for Portugal in 23 different years, which is longer than most players’ international careers – let alone for them to be consistently delivering in front of goal.

Even if a player was to make their international debut at 18, they’d have to play into their forties and score without fail every year to surpass Ronaldo’s record.

Ronaldo might have missed out on the all-time World Cup scoring record, but he does boast the most goals at European Championships.

His final tally of 14 is five higher than the next best, Michel Platini on nine.

Alvaro Morata and Kane have seven each, but will be 35 and 34 by the time the next Euros start. Kane could give him a run for his money, but he might only have one more shot at it. If the next Euros are his last, he’d need to score eight times to beat Ronaldo’s record – something only Platini has done at a single Euros tournament before.

Ronaldo is one of only two outfield players to make more than 20 Euros appearances, so if anyone wants to threaten his goalscoring record, their ratio is going to have to be high.

As stated, Ronaldo played in 30 Euros matches. It only takes seven games to go from the group stage all the way to the final, so someone would have to do that four times and more to beat his record.

UEFA have previously put plans to expand the Euros on the backburner, so someone would need to have a near-20-year international career – and do well in it – to come close to Ronaldo’s record.

Ronaldo and Messi are the only outfield players to go to six World Cups – and Ronaldo scored at every one he went to.

Unless the World Cup becomes a more frequent event than one that happens every four years, someone would have to play for two decades, always qualify for the World Cup and always score at it to match Ronaldo’s record, let alone beat it.

How Arsenal have decided the title race before winter has even arrived - the Gunners dine alone this season

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Arsenal are champions of the autumn, and winter could prove to be their wonderland

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Title drama isn’t guaranteed every season. See Manchester City 2017/18 and Liverpool 2019/20 for a procession of one.

Nevertheless, there are usually a few honeymoon months for broadcasters and fans to dream of a proper footrace before the strongest on the savannah hunt down the antelopes. Super Sunday has to have meaning, right?

Jose Mourinho claimed that Chelsea were “champions of the autumn” when his side defeated Liverpool to sit top of the tree just before the November international break in 2014. The Blues had only dropped four points in 11 matches. The Portuguese said: “I think we have a very good chance to win the title, but I think we, Arsenal, Manchester City, Manchester United and Southampton are all still in the race.” Southampton. Hmmm.

Jose was bluffing, bigging up rivals who weren’t in the same ballpark. His side cantered over the line.

Man City and Arsenal were the closest challengers in that campaign, losing seven matches to Chelsea’s three. Manchester United were in a post-Ferguson funk, and Brendan Rodgers was experiencing the last rites as Anfield manager.

The 2025/26 class has a similar issue in the quality of challengers. Enzo Maresca’s New Model Army blasted out of the blocks with seven straight wins but have been hobbled by Pep’s familiar foes in Brighton and Aston Villa. It may be that the looming prospect of losing points through other means is beginning to sink deeper into their psyche.

Those 114 charges aren’t so much the elephant in the room as the rat in the kitchen. Arsenal are dining for one right now. They are master chefs while the rest are still trying to find the ingredients to lay an oven glove at the top table.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Liverpool’s loss to the champions last Sunday appeared to deflate the Anfield air and any sense that the Reds might be pretenders to the throne. Truth be told, no one had Andoni Iraola’s expensively imbalanced squad as a serious contender after the problematic 2025/26 season. They didn't have a shot on target and fell nine points behind the leaders. Just one look at Jamie Carragher's face told the story.

Chelsea, meanwhile, took the lead at the Emirates, but haven’t really clicked into anything resembling a stride - at least not against title-contender opposition.

The games that Arsenal were drawing in 2024/25 - and to a lesser extent last season - are now being won. There is a seismic gap between the sheer efficiency of the Gunners and the rest of the pack. That efficiency now has extra security and titanium screws in all areas barring the forward line. The midfield monster that is Bruno Guimaraes roars whenever a tackle is made. Ezri Konsa has slotted in as if he knew William Saliba’s every move. Missing Bukayo Saka? It’s not a big deal anymore.

Arsenal know who they are and are expanding their limited attacking horizons without losing that deadlock bolt at the back. The lack of a striker isn’t a problem when Gabriel and Konsa can win a game at both ends. The North London team have been victorious in 14 out of their last 15 Premier League matches .

While Xabi Alonso and Iraola are trying to restore order, Mikel Arteta’s squad are in absolute lockstep.

During that hard-fought victory over Sunderland in September, a game that Arsenal would surely have drawn in previous incarnations, Carragher mooted that Arteta was trying to become the new Sir Alex of the Premier League. The Spaniard hasn't quite got the caustic asides, dry wit or trophy haul of the legendary Scot, but the war machine is heavily stocked. Nothing escapes Arteta’s mind map if it means marginal gains. He is controlling the Wild West. “I have a massive storage of images.” Quite.

The Premier League will celebrate its 35th champion next year and, to be brutally honest, it is searching for its reinvention as an entertainment package. The coalface clashes between highly decorated clubs have felt lukewarm for a while now, like gentle sparring from former greats on a senior tour.

Squeaky bum time in May seems fanciful. Arteta has learned that the W column is all that counts. When Hull City turn up at the Emirates without a win in seven, the Gunners will look to hit the 27-point mark, one behind Jose’s highly drilled warriors 12 years ago. "I know that the negative moment will arrive, the defeat will arrive, and we are prepared for that,” said Mourinho.

It could be a long winter for the chasing pack when that late November to March cycle restarts after the Nations League. Arsenal 2.0 are bulletproof.

Aluminium canopy encases waterfront pavilion in Providence by Architecture Research Office

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Steel columns support the expansive canopy of this green-roofed pavilion at 195 District Park in Providence, Rhode Island, which has been completed by New York-based studio Architecture Research Office.

Conceived by the studio as a “year-round amenity” for the public, the 195 District Park Pavilion accommodates two local businesses, an indoor-outdoor dining area and public restrooms overlooking the Providence River.

The pavilion’s position at the park’s highest point led Architecture Research Office (ARO) to design its multifaceted structure as a “beacon” that would be accessible from multiple entrances.

“Centrally located within 195 District Park, the pavilion is a park amenity that welcomes and orients visitors while supporting park programming,” studio principal Kim Yao told Dezeen.

“The pavilion is located at the highest point within the park in response to the floodplain of the adjacent Providence River.”

“This positioning created an opportunity for the building to serve as a beacon, visible from multiple entry points to the park,” Yao added.

Fronted by a plaza, the pavilion is composed of a metal frame that has been cloaked with cedar wood in a nod to the area’s local architecture.

Its structure is wrapped by an expansive aluminium canopy, which is supported by a series of steel columns and extends over the plaza to shelter an outdoor seating space.

Atop the structure, the sloped green roof helps to blend the pavilion into its surrounding landscape, while also limiting stormwater run-off and screening the rooftop’s mechanical systems.

“As a year-round amenity, the pavilion opens to shaded exterior areas in the summer while providing shelter during colder seasons,” Yao said.

“It also provides occupants with views of the river and surrounding areas,” she added.

Inside, the 3,500-square-foot building opens up to the indoor seating area, complete with exposed wooden joists.

Flanking the space are the pavilion’s operable storefront doors, which open up to the adjacent plaza and enable passive ventilation throughout the space.

Additional facilities include public restrooms located at the pavilion’s southern corner.

Its structure is perched beside a curved pedestrian bridge completed by Inform Studio and Buro Happold in 2020 that stretches over the Providence River to link two parks on the east and west sides of the city’s riverfront.

Other pavilions recently featured on Dezeen include a temporary marketing space in São Paulo built from mass-timber scrap and a basket-like aluminium pavilion in New York.

Architects: Architecture Research OfficeLandscape architect: Agency Landscape + PlanningStructural engineer: Hatfield GroupMEP engineer: ColladoLighting designer: Lighting WorkshopCivil engineer: Fuss & O’NeillGeotechnical engineer: RMA EnvironmentalPlacemaking and food service: AgoraOwner’s representative: ColliersConstruction manager: Shawmut Design and Construction

Micro-Investing App Fees – $4 a Month Costs 9.6% a Year on $500

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The credit card, banking, and other financial offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us – for example, when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

To see what a micro-investing app’s flat monthly fee costs you, multiply it by 12 and divide by your balance. On a small balance, that yearly percentage can be close to or above the 10% a year large U.S. stocks have averaged since 1928. Until it falls under 1%, invest through a broker with no account fee.

Acorns charges $4, $8, or $12 a month, depending on the plan, and Stash’s plan costs $12 a month, or $108 for a year paid up front. Those prices stay the same whatever your balance, so they take the biggest percentage of a small one.

At $4 a month, you pay $48 a year, which is 9.6% of a $500 balance. That 9.6% is nearly all of the 10% a year the S&P 500, an index of 500 large U.S. companies, has returned on average since 1928, according to data from NYU professor Aswath Damodaran.

Index stock mutual funds, which track an index like the S&P 500, charged an average fee of 0.05% a year in 2025, the Investment Company Institute found. On the same $500, that’s 25 cents a year against the app’s $48.

When you do the math, count every balance the fee covers, the app’s retirement account included, not just its regular investing account. A $12 monthly fee on $2,000 across both is $144 a year, or 7.2%.

Round-ups, which invest the spare change from each purchase, can be worth the fee while they’re the only way you’d invest at all. You can build the same habit for free, though. Check what your round-ups added last month, then set up an automatic monthly investment of that amount at an online broker with no account fee. Pick one that sells fractional shares, so even $20 buys a slice of a fund that tracks the S&P 500.

The fee drops below 1% a year, a tenth of the S&P 500’s 10% average, once your balance reaches 1,200 times the monthly fee. That’s a balance of $4,800 on a $4 plan and $14,400 on a $12 plan.

To set up that automatic investment, compare the best online brokers.

Review: Titanium Court (Switch 2) - The Freakiest Dream You've Ever Had

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It sounds like the stuff of dreams. Fever dreams. The theatre audience is waiting, and you’re in the wings. Without warning, an MC announces that the lead actress is unavailable, so you’re thrust onstage as understudy – but without a clue what you’re supposed to do. A solicitor’s billboard.

Titanium Court, the creation of New York-based developer AP Thomson, is driven by a singular vision. It mashes up almost anything it can get its hands on – whether it’s different game genres, different musical styles, or just different images of sports or snappy tailoring or the Highway Code. Since the only common thread seems to be the developer’s mind, it feels strangely personal despite the miscellaneous compilation of ideas jumping out of nowhere. A man in a suit playing a trumpet.

Looking at screenshots, you’ll think this is a match-three game – and it sort of is: it’s a strategic, roguelike, narrative-driven match-three game. In fact, the match-three game seems as surprised to be there as you are. Having been dropped into the theatrical setting, you soon establish that your role in the drama is that of the faerie queen, ruling (cluelessly at first) over your royal court. Said court sits in a terrain of matchable tiles that can each be swapped with adjacent ones to make three in a row. Matched tiles disappear and anything sitting above them falls down the screen, with new tiles appearing from the top to fill any gaps. Depending on what you match, you will receive resources of different kinds – food, stone, wood or water. As you make moves, a bar depletes, freezing the terrain once it runs out. A pair of sunglasses.

That whole match-three-ing phase is called 'high tide'. Once 'low tide' arrives, the court goes to war. The terrain you’ve done your best to design becomes the battlefield. Usually, there are enemy fortifications on it, and you must spend your resources to be ready to deal with them by training troops or workers or setting up defences. However, this is all part of a larger campaign, so resources should be strategically preserved for subsequent rounds. A basketball swishing the net.

Between runs, you are returned to your faerie court, where mystery unfolds to an eerily wistful tune. These sections are presented in a top-down view of the royal residence, with icons to select your actions: visit the dining hall, talk to your aide, take a shower, etc. The story is told here by the characters in the play — or perhaps the cast — always keeping logic just about at arm’s length. The repetition of the high-and-low-tide gather-and-hunt routine is consistently fun, but it’s the in-betweens that provide pacing, a narrative arc, and an opportunity to put the game down until your next chapter, breaking up the addictive loops common to match-threes. A rock star smashing their guitar.

Through all of this, it’s not clear whether your wide-eyed confusion is part of your role. Are the other characters helping you according to the script, or are the actors out of character, helping you to understand the script? But the script is unfinished anyway, the MC announces up front: the writers are racing you to the ending. Even your tooltip is in on the whole charade, personified in dialogue with you. The overall effect is something bizarrely coherent. It’s a jumble of a concept but, wrapped in the framing device of the theatre, nothing can technically be out of place. A 'No Parking' sign.

If we try and strip it all down to the gameplay essentials, Titanium Court has a moreish tension going on. The strategising grows in sophistication the more you play, and the goals of the game get clearer. However, those goals always lie on the other side of the randomness of the tile-matching mechanics. The balance of skill and luck is just about right, and complemented by the interspersed court segments. Success feels like your own achievement, while failure — although sometimes due to cruel tile-generation — affords an opportunity to relax in the court and explore its eccentricities – or who knows what might happen, frankly? A sticking-plaster.

So there’s a solid game under all the theatrics, but the theatrics are what will grab and hold your attention. The writing is thoughtful without pontificating, funny without leaning on jokes, and adventurous without losing sight of its absurdity. Perhaps the most absurd thing is how naturally this gels with a genre so far removed from Titanium Court’s philosophical ambitions. You could argue that the repetitive parts of the game wear out their welcome – but that’s not before the meta game gives you an out. You can play again and again if you like, but this is a controlled story that has an opinion about how and when it should end. It wouldn’t be fair to judge it on what comes after the curtain call. A glass of wine.

The graphical style also works wonderfully. Despite the tight colour palette on a dark screen, things jump to life. The eye-catching, whimsical images that pop up will bounce or fly or slide across the window in ways that fit the action perfectly. The thrill of an out-of-nowhere, pixel-art touchdown as the battle music rocks out is just perfect. A dog catching a frisbee.

Theatrical performance, narrative adventure, strategy game, match-three… Shuffling its deck of faerie queens and love potions and slam dunks and Ray-Bans, Titanium Court is the freakiest dream you’ve ever had. It might baffle your psychoanalyst, but that’s no reason to wake up.

Why Running Slower Can Make You a Faster Runner

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Runners love to push themselves. But holding back can help create a stronger base, especially when you’re training for a big race.

Slowing down can actually help you get faster.

On this week’s episode of The Amazing Runner’s World Show, Aly and Kristine discuss why slow runs deserve a crucial spot in your training plan.

In the previous episode, we talked about how to pace the five boroughs of the NYC Marathon.

Kristine Kearns, a writer and avid runner, joined Runner’s World and Bicycling in July 2024. She previously coached high school girls cross country and currently competes in seasonal races, with more than six years of distance training and an affinity for weightlifting. You can find her wearing purple, baking cupcakes, and visiting her local farmers market.

Aly is the Director of Content Hype at Runner’s World. She started running over a decade ago and has run 8 marathons, plus hundreds of shorter distance races. When she isn't running, she loves watching reality competition shows, reading and curating the perfect Taylor Swift playlist.

Mary Earps reveals birth of ‘secret’ baby boy with partner Kitty Forrest

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Former England Lioness Mary Earps has revealed the birth of a ‘secret’ baby boy with her partner Kitty Forrest.

Earps revealed a year ago that she was in a same-sex relationship with Forest but had not disclosed any details about the pregnancy until a surprise announcement on Friday.

‘I’ve been keeping a little secret from you all, and I don’t think I can talk my way out of this one, so I’m going to have to show you,’ Earps said at the start of her video on YouTube.

At the end of the video, Earps revealed that ‘Baby Forrest Earps’ was born on November 5.

Sign up to our daily football newsletter, pick your team and get the latest news sent directly to your inbox every morning.

In a separate announcement on Friday evening, Earps revealed her son’s name is Xander.

In her Instagram post, Earps wrote: ‘I can’t quite believe I’m writing this, but yesterday Kitty and I welcomed our beautiful baby boy into the world!

‘We’re absolutely over the moon and still trying to take it all in. It’s been such an emotional journey getting to this point, and we couldn’t be more excited to begin this next chapter of our lives as a family of three.

‘Already a full-time footballer, and now a full-time mum too! There’s so much I’m looking forward to sharing with you all as I navigate life in football, motherhood and everything in between. I can’t wait to bring you along for the ride!

‘For now, we’re soaking up every second of our little bubble.

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

‘Thank you for all the love and support along the way. It means more than you know, and I’m so excited to share this new adventure with you all.

‘Baby name to be revealed! Our greatest adventure starts here.’

Earps, who retired from international football in May last year, returned to England in the summer to sign for London City Lionesses having spent the last two seasons with Paris Saint-Germain.

The 33-year-old goalkeeper had played an integral role in the Lionesses’ success at Euro 2022.

Earps also won the Women’s Finalissima, the SheBelieves Cup and the Arnold Clark Cup twice during her time with England, while she was also in goal for the Lionesses defeat to Spain in the World Cup final in 2023.

Why West Brom’s £2m summer addition is becoming a smart signing under James Morrison

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

West Brom missed their chance to move into the top two as they drew 1-1 with Southampton on Tuesday night. As they prepare to host Millwall this weekend, James Morrison‘s WBA are serious about fighting for promotion this season, winning three of their last five including an impressive 3-1 win over West Ham at the London Stadium.

The start that they have had comes off the back of a successful summer window. Under Morrison, WBA made a real effort to revamp the squad with more youth and several new additions were made with that in mind.

Jimmy-Jay Morgan has taken a lot of the plaudits after all his goalscoring exploits. However, another one of their additions is starting to break into the side, Rabby Nzingoula.

The Baggies spent a reported £2m on the 20-year-old after a decent second half of the season on loan at Nurnberg. He has been a hugely important player over the past five games where West Brom have gone unbeaten.

The 20-year-old arrived from Strasbourg with plenty of potential but relatively little experience of English football. However, three months into his Hawthorns career, he is adapting to the Championship quicker than many would have expected.

Nzingoula has gradually moved from an impact substitute to a regular part of James Morrison’s midfield, making 14 appearances and accumulating 710 minutes. Seven of those outings have been starts, while his average rating of 6.73 suggests he is providing a dependable contribution despite his young age.

There is also more to his game than simply breaking up opposition attacks. Nzingoula has recorded 41 line-breaking passes and created nine chances in his Championship minutes so far. His 1.14 chances created per 90 puts him in the 75th percentile, while his shot attempts rank in the 80th percentile.

These figures suggest West Brom have a midfielder capable of contributing further up the pitch rather than simply protecting the defence.

At 20 years old, Nzingoula has a good level of experience at decent levels. The WBA man has played 37 times in Ligue 1 as well as featuirng in Europe for Strasbourg.

His versatility is another positive. Comfortable operating as either a six or an eight, Nzingoula gives Morrison different options in central and wider midfield areas. His recent performances, including a 7.3 rating against Southampton on November 3, suggest he is beginning to find his feet.

There are still areas for improvement. He has yet to register a goal or assist, his aerial numbers are relatively modest and three yellow cards highlight the occasional risks associated with his aggressive approach.

Nevertheless, West Brom should be encouraged. Nzingoula is only 20, has already demonstrated Championship-level defensive qualities and is showing signs of becoming a more complete midfielder. For a reported £2m, the Baggies could have secured a very smart long-term investment.
